# Editor & Scene Format

This document records the scope decision for NSGo's game editor ([#159](https://github.com/nsgo-dev/game/issues/159)) and specifies the version 1 YAML scene format ([#160](https://github.com/nsgo-dev/game/issues/160)) implemented in `github.com/nsgo-dev/game/pkg/sceneformat`.

Prebuilt desktop installers for **NSGo Game Editor** (`cmd/editor`)—signed and notarized `.dmg` images for macOS (`arm64` / `x64`), `-setup.exe` installers for Windows (`x64` / `arm64`), and `.deb` / `.pkg.tar.gz` / `.AppImage` packages for Linux (`x64` / `arm64`)—are published on [GitHub Releases](https://github.com/nsgo-dev/game/releases/latest), alongside one-line installers (`install.sh` / `install.ps1`). The standalone editor requires **Go 1.27 or later** ([go.dev/dl](https://go.dev/dl/)) to create projects, play games, run checks, and build exports; when opened from Finder, the Dock, Spotlight, or a desktop launcher, it resolves `go` from `PATH`, standard install locations (`/usr/local/go/bin`, `C:\Program Files\Go\bin`), Homebrew, version-manager shims, and your login shell's `PATH`. You can also launch the editor directly via `go tool game editor` or `go run ./cmd/editor`.

## Editor Scope Decision (#159)

NSGo takes **option (b) growing into (c)**: keep Go code and Tiled first-class, define a clean human-editable YAML scene format shared by hand-authored scenes and tooling, and build a lightweight NSGo-native scene editor on top of it.

### Why this scope

- **Code and Tiled stay first-class.** A game written purely in Go—or authored with Tiled `.tmj` maps and TexturePacker/Aseprite atlases—never has to go through an editor. `sceneformat` is an ordinary library package with zero runtime lock-in.
- **One text format, readable diffs.** Scenes are plain versioned YAML (`version: 1`) with deterministic 2-space encoding. Hand-editing a scene in a text editor and saving it from a visual editor produce the same diff-friendly structure.
- **Dogfooding NSGo.** Rather than wrapping a third-party desktop shell or building a monolithic IDE, the lightweight scene editor is an NSGo app built with NSGo's own `ui` views and `Canvas`. Every improvement to inspectors, tree views, shortcuts, or canvas tooling benefits NSGo itself.

### Planned lightweight editor capabilities

The NSGo-native editor operates directly on `sceneformat.SceneDoc` files and focuses on the workflows where visual feedback beats hand-editing coordinates:

1. **Object placement & transforms**: place, drag, rotate, scale, flip and Z-order objects across world and screen layers.
2. **Tile painting**: paint inline tile grids, and the tile layers of layered grids with flipped tiles, or preview referenced Tiled `.tmj` and LDtk `.ldtk` maps.
3. **Typed property & component editing**: inspect and edit `components` and `properties` with validation against the scene schema.
4. **Undo / redo**: command-based editing history over scene documents.
5. **Play-in-editor**: run the live `game.Loop` and `BuiltScene` directly inside the editor's `Canvas` without leaving the tool.
6. **Asset browsing**: browse project atlases, frames, animations and tilemaps and drag them into layers.

Out of scope for the initial editor: custom scripting languages, bespoke shader/material graphs, and proprietary binary project formats.

---

## Scene Format (`version: 4`) (#160)

```go
import "github.com/nsgo-dev/game/pkg/sceneformat"
```

### Top-level fields

| Field | Type | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `version` | `int` | Required schema version: `4` (`CurrentVersion`), `3` for a scene using version 3 parallax/repeat layers without version 4 3D lights/environment/cameras, `2` for version 2 features without version 3/4 fields, or `1` for none of the fields added in versions 2–4. `Encode` writes it back unchanged. |
| `uid` | `string` | Optional stable ID that stays the same when the file is renamed or moved, written by `go tool game index` (all versions; see [Stable references](project.md#stable-references)). `Resolve` keeps the scene's own uid, not its base's. |
| `inherits` | `string` | Optional slash-separated path from the project root to a base scene this one inherits (version 2+, see Instancing and inheritance below). |
| `name` | `string` | Optional human-readable scene name. |
| `navMesh` | `string` | Optional slash-separated path from the project root to the scene's baked [navigation mesh file](navbake3d.md), which `go tool game navmesh` writes (all versions). In a scene that inherits another, it replaces the base's. |
| `virtualWidth`, `virtualHeight` | `float32` | Virtual viewport size in points. Must both be `0` or both positive. |
| `backgroundColor` | `string` | Optional CSS color string parsed by `core.ParseColor` (for example `"#141824"`). Filled over `(0, 0, virtualWidth, virtualHeight)` before layers draw. |
| `ambientColor` | `string` | Optional CSS color string parsed by `core.ParseColor` (for example `"rgba(12, 16, 30, 0.85)"`). Sets the ambient darkness tint of the scene's 2D lighting pass (`lights2d.Scene.Ambient`, both versions). |
| `camera` | object | Optional default 2D camera for world-space layers (`x`, `y`, `zoom`, `rotation`, optional `bounds`). |
| `environment` | object | Optional 3D scene environment (version 4): `sky` (`kind`: `color`, `gradient`, or `hdr` with `color` / `zenith`, `horizon`, `ground` / `hdr` path), `ambientColor`, `ambientIntensity`, `iblIntensity`, `fog` (`mode`: `off`, `linear`, `exp`, `exp2`; `color`, `start`, `end`, `density`, `maxOpacity`, height fog `heightEnabled`, `heightBase`, `heightFalloff`, `heightDensity`), `tonemapping` (`none`, `reinhard`, `aces`, `neutral`, `agx`), and `exposure`. |
| `tilemaps` | map | Optional named tilemaps referenced from layers via `tilemap.ref`. |
| `layerOverrides` | map | Changes to inherited layers by name: `space`, `order`, `visible`, `tilemap` (version 2+), plus `parallaxX`, `parallaxY`, `repeatX`, `repeatY`, `repeatWidth`, `repeatHeight` (version 3, requires `inherits`). |
| `overrides` | map | Changes to inherited objects by ID (version 2+, requires `inherits`). |
| `layers` | list | Ordered list of layers drawn lowest `order` first, preserving document order on ties. |
| `connections` | list | Optional connections from one object's events to another object's handlers, written after the layers (all versions, see [Connections](#connections-490)). |

### Camera

- `x`, `y`: world coordinates placed at the center of the virtual viewport `(virtualWidth/2, virtualHeight/2)` (or `(0, 0)` when virtual dimensions are `0`).
- `zoom`: positive zoom factor; defaults to `1` when omitted.
- `rotation`: camera rotation in radians (clockwise on screen); world layers rotate by `-rotation` around `(x, y)`.
- `bounds`: optional `{x, y, width, height}` world rectangle clamping the visible area (`width > 0`, `height > 0`).
- When `camera` is omitted, world layers draw with the identity transform.

### Tilemaps

A tilemap entry (in top-level `tilemaps` or inline on a layer) uses exactly one mode:

- **Reference** (`ref`, layers only): names an entry in top-level `tilemaps`.
- **External map** (`source`): a slash-separated path from the project root to a Tiled map (`.tmj` or `.tmx`) or an LDtk project (`.ldtk`); for LDtk, `level` names the level to build (version 2), and without it the project's first level is built.
- **Inline grid**: `tileWidth`, `tileHeight`, `width`, `height`, either `tileset` (atlas name whose frames are keyed `"1"`, `"2"`, …) or `tilesetFile` (version 2+, path of a YAML tileset `*.tileset.yaml`), optional `offsetX` / `offsetY` (version 3, shifting cell `(0, 0)` in layer points), and row-major `data` of length `width * height` (`0` is an empty tile; when using `tilesetFile`, ID `n` places local tile `n - 1`).
- **Layered grid** (version 2): `tileWidth`, `tileHeight`, `width`, `height`, optional `offsetX` / `offsetY` (version 3), `tilesetFile`, the path of a YAML tileset (`*.tileset.yaml`, see [YAML tilesets](tilemap.md#yaml-tilesets)) whose tiles the cells place, and `tileLayers`, up to `MaxTileLayers` named layers drawn in order.

The tileset of a layered grid defines each tile once, with its flags, properties, collision shapes, animation and terrain, so the map only places tiles, as a Godot TileSet does for its TileMapLayers. Each tile layer has a `name`, optional `visible` (default `true`) and `opacity` (default `1`), and `rows`: `height` rows from the top, each `width` cells separated by spaces. A cell is `.` when empty, or the tile's local ID in the tileset (`0`, `1`, …) followed by any of `h`, `v` and `d` to flip it horizontally, vertically or across its diagonal, as Tiled's flip bits do, so `3hv` is tile 3 turned half a turn. A row may align its cells with extra spaces and write the letters in any order; `Encode` writes single spaces and the order `h`, `v`, `d`, so each row is one line in a diff.

```yaml
# yaml-language-server: $schema=https://game.nsgo.dev/schemas/scene.v2.json
version: 2
name: Cave
virtualWidth: 320
virtualHeight: 180
layers:
  - name: ground
    tilemap:
      tileWidth: 16
      tileHeight: 16
      width: 4
      height: 2
      tilesetFile: tilesets/cave.tileset.yaml
      tileLayers:
        - name: floor
          rows:
            - 0 0 0 0
            - . . 1h 3hvd
        - name: deco
          opacity: 0.5
          rows:
            - 2 . . .
            - . . . .
```

`TileLayerDoc.Cell`, `SetCell` and `Cells` read and write a layer's cells as tile values, the GIDs they have once built: `0` for an empty cell, otherwise the local ID plus 1 with `tilemap.FlipHorizontal`, `FlipVertical` and `FlipDiagonal` set; `ParseTileRow` and `FormatTileRow` do the same for one row.

`Build` turns a single grid of atlas frames into a sprite batch (`BuiltLayer.Tilemap`), and a layered grid, an inline grid with `tilesetFile`, or an external map into a `tilemap.Tilemap` (`BuiltLayer.Map`). The map carries the tileset's flags, properties, shapes, animations and terrains, so in the layer's coordinates it answers `FlagsAt`, `TileProperties`, `TilesInRect` and `Raycast`, collides directly with `physics.MoveAndSlide`, and serves `tileshape.New` for slopes and occluders. `BuiltScene.Draw` draws only the map tiles inside the virtual viewport, seen through the camera for a world-space layer, unless the scene has no virtual size or `DrawAllTiles` is set, as the editor sets it for its edit view; `BuiltScene.Update(dt)` advances animated tiles.

`Build` reads YAML tilesets, Tiled maps with their external tilesets and LDtk projects through the resolver's `FileResolver` (`ReadFile`), with paths in a Tiled or LDtk file taken from that file's directory, and loads their images through an `ImageResolver` (`Image`) when the resolver has one, or names them by path (`core.ImageSource{URI: path}`) otherwise. `FSAssets` reads a project's files from an `fs.FS`, so a scene that names a `.tmj`, `.ldtk` or `.tileset.yaml` builds with no resolver of the game's own. A resolver that implements `TilemapResolver` still loads every `source` itself into a `TilemapDoc`, as before, except a source that names an LDtk `level`: `Tilemap(source)` cannot receive the level, so `Build` reads that one through the `FileResolver`.

### Layers and Objects

Each layer has:

- `name` (required, unique within the scene)
- `space`: `"world"` (default when omitted, draws under camera) or `"screen"` (draws in canvas coordinates, ignoring camera)
- `order`: integer draw order (lowest first)
- `visible`: boolean, defaults to `true` when omitted
- `parallaxX`, `parallaxY` (version 3): optional finite scroll factors applied to the camera position on `"world"` layers, matching `scene.Layer`; omitted defaults to `1`, `0` pins the layer against camera translation, `0.5` scrolls at half speed, and values above `1` scroll faster for foregrounds. `layerOverrides` can override them on inherited scenes.
- `repeatX`, `repeatY` (version 3): optional booleans that tile a `"world"` layer seamlessly across the camera's visible region using `repeatWidth` / `repeatHeight` (non-negative layer points; `0` falls back to the layer's tilemap pixel size, then the scene's virtual viewport size). `layerOverrides` can override them on inherited scenes.
- `tilemap`: optional tilemap drawn beneath the layer's objects
- `objects`: placed objects in the layer

Each object has:

- `id` (required, unique across the entire scene)
- `name`, `prefab` (optional)
- Transform: `x`, `y`, `originX`, `originY`, `rotation` (radians, clockwise), `scaleX` (default `1`), `scaleY` (default `1`), `flipX`, `flipY`, `alpha` (`[0, 1]`, default `1`), `z` (layer-local draw order, lowest first)
- `sprite`: optional `{atlas, frame}` referencing a `sprite.Atlas` and frame name
- `particles`: optional `{file, atlas}` (version 2) placing a 2D particle emitter YAML file (`file` is a slash-separated path from the project root; optional `atlas` resolves the emitter's `frameNames`). `sceneformat.Build` loads `file` through `ParticlesResolver` when implemented, falling back to `FileResolver` + `particles.UnmarshalConfigYAML` (so `FSAssets` works automatically), creates `BuiltObject.Emitter` (collected in `BuiltLayer.Emitters`), positions it at the object's composed world transform (updated by `BuiltObject.Sync`), advances it in `BuiltScene.Update(dt)`, and draws it in Z-order interleaved with sprites in `BuiltScene.Draw(d)`
- `material`: optional slash-separated path from the project root to a shared material file, such as `materials/stone.material.yaml`, resolved at build time through `MaterialResolver` (see [resource](resources.md))
- 3D look: `mesh`, one of the built-in primitives `box`, `sphere`, `plane`, `capsule` and `cylinder`, `model` (version 2), the slash-separated path from the project root to a glTF 2.0 file (`.gltf` or `.glb`), optionally narrowed to one of its nodes by `modelNode` or one of its meshes by `modelMesh`, or `gridMap` (version 2), the slash-separated path from the project root to a `*.gridmap.yaml` / `*.gridmap.yml` file; an object's `material` replaces the model's or grid map's materials or gives the primitive its surface; optional `occluder` (version 2) marks the object as a 3D occluder (`mode: model` or `mode: box` with optional `center`, `size`, and `doubleSided`). `scenemodel.Build` turns them into geometry, LODs, and occluders ([scenemodel](scenemodel.md)). `CheckObjectModel` checks these fields on one object, and the editor's `PlaceObject` refuses an object it refuses; the inspector's Mesh and GridMap fields are mutually exclusive with each other and with `model`
- 3D light (`light`, version 4): optional 3D light attached to the object's transform (`kind`: `directional`, `point`, or `spot`; CSS `color`, `intensity`, `range`, spot `innerCone` and `outerCone` half-angles in degrees, and `shadows`). Directional and spot lights aim along the object's local `-Z` axis. `CheckObjectLight` validates one object's light.
- 3D camera (`camera`, version 4): optional 3D camera attached to the object's transform (`projection`: `perspective` or `orthographic`, `fov` in degrees or `orthoHeight`, `near`, `far`, and `current`). At most one 3D camera in a scene may set `current: true`. `CheckObjectCamera3D` validates one object's camera.
- `components`: optional mapping from a component type name to its fields; free-form unless a component registry is given (see [Component types](#component-types))
- `properties`: optional `map[string]any` for custom metadata
- `scene`: optional slash-separated path from the project root to a scene file this object instances (version 2, see Instancing and inheritance below)
- `overrides`: optional changes to the instanced scene's objects by ID (version 2, requires `scene`)
- `children`: optional list of objects attached to this one (see Node tree below)

### Node tree (#484)

An object can hold child objects under `children`, nested up to `MaxObjectDepth` (64) levels. A child's transform is local to its parent: `x`, `y`, `rotation`, `scaleX` and `scaleY` (and in 3D `posZ`, `rotX`, `rotY`, `rotZ` and `scaleZ`) compose with every ancestor's, and its `alpha` multiplies theirs. `originX`, `originY`, `flipX` and `flipY` stay the object's own, and `z` orders the whole layer, children included, with ties drawing each parent before its children. Children belong to their parent's layer, and their IDs share the scene-wide namespace, so an ID used anywhere in the tree is refused anywhere else.

```yaml
layers:
  - name: ships
    objects:
      - id: ship
        x: 100
        y: 50
        rotation: 1.5707964
        scaleX: 2
        scaleY: 2
        sprite: { atlas: actors, frame: hull }
        children:
          - id: turret
            x: 10        # lands at (100, 70): a quarter turn and twice the size of ship
            sprite: { atlas: actors, frame: turret }
            children:
              - id: barrel
                x: 5     # lands at (100, 80)
```

The same tree loads three ways:

- `Build` links each `BuiltObject` to its `Parent` and `Children`, sets `World` to the composed 2D affine transform and `WorldAlpha` to the composed alpha, and draws each child's sprite at its world position, rotation and scale. A sprite whose composed transform is sheared (a rotated child under a non-uniformly scaled parent) cannot be drawn as a sprite, so `Validate` refuses it, and with it `Parse`, `Encode` and `Build`; `CheckObjectTree` makes the same check, and the nesting limit, for a single tree. A parent collapsed to zero size along one axis (such as `scaleX: 0`) is not a shear: its child sprites draw at zero scale. Objects without a sprite keep the exact matrix in `World`. `BuiltLayer.Objects` lists a layer's top-level objects, and `BuiltScene.Objects` indexes every object by ID. After changing a built object's document fields, `BuiltObject.Sync` recomposes its `World` and `WorldAlpha` and its sprite, and its descendants', and reports false for a sprite the change would shear.
- `SpawnEntities(doc, world)` spawns one entity per object into an `entities.World`, parented in its `Hierarchy2D` as the objects nest, with the object's local `Transform2D` and an `ObjectRef` component naming the object and its layer. The top-level objects of a hidden layer spawn hidden, so their subtrees are not world-visible. Pass a registry via `SpawnEntitiesWith(doc, world, SpawnOptions{Components: reg})` to validate and resolve every component up front and invoke each registered `ComponentType.Spawn` factory on its entity (rolling back entities created so far on error).
- `BuildNodes3D(doc, scene.Root())` builds a `scene3d` node tree: one group node per layer (carrying the layer's visibility), and one node per object named by its ID, with `UserData` pointing at its `ObjectDoc`. `ObjectLocalTransform3D` gives each node's local transform; `rotX`, `rotY` and `rotZ` apply in that order about fixed axes, the same order the editor's 3D viewport uses.
- `scenemodel.Build(doc, scene.Root(), assets)` builds the same tree and attaches to each object's node the geometry and materials of its `mesh`, `model` or `gridMap`, with the node's `LocalBounds` set from the geometry, ready for `render3d.Queue` ([scenemodel](scenemodel.md)).

```go
spawned, err := sceneformat.SpawnEntities(doc, world)
if err != nil {
	return err
}
world.Hierarchy2D().Sync()
wt, _ := world.Hierarchy2D().WorldTransform(spawned["barrel"]) // wt.X == 100, wt.Y == 80

nodes, err := sceneformat.BuildNodes3D(doc, scene.Root())
if err != nil {
	return err
}
pos := nodes.Objects["barrel"].WorldTransform().Pos
```

Moving, deleting or undoing an object in the editor carries its children along, and the editor never hands out an ID that a nested child already uses. It refuses to place a tree deeper than `MaxObjectDepth` or holding a sheared sprite, and refuses a scale or rotation that would shear a sprite, the object's own or a descendant's, under the transforms of its ancestors. Both viewports draw, pick and edit nested children, and the [scene tree](#scene-tree-861) selects, reparents, reorders and renames them.

### Instancing and inheritance (#483)

A version 2 scene reuses other scene files the way Godot's scenes do. Every reference is a slash-separated path from the project root, like a material's, and `Parse` and `Encode` keep references and overrides exactly as written: a level of instances saves as the same instances, never as copies of their objects.

An object with `scene` instances that file. The top-level objects of every layer of the instanced scene, in document order, become the object's first children, ahead of its own `children`, so the object's transform places the whole instance. Their IDs are prefixed with the object's ID and a slash, so ten instances of one enemy hold `enemy_1/body` to `enemy_10/body`. `overrides` changes the instanced objects, keyed by their IDs inside the instanced scene; a scene instanced inside it is reached by its prefixed ID, such as `gun/barrel`. The instanced scene's layer settings, camera, background and virtual size do not carry over, and a scene whose layers hold a tilemap cannot be instanced.

```yaml
# scenes/enemy.scene.yaml
version: 2
name: Enemy
layers:
  - name: root
    objects:
      - id: body
        sprite: { atlas: actors, frame: hero }
        properties: { health: 3, speed: 40 }
        children:
          - id: torch
            x: 6
            sprite: { atlas: actors, frame: torch }

# level.scene.yaml
version: 2
layers:
  - name: actors
    objects:
      - id: enemy_1
        x: 32
        "y": 40
        scene: scenes/enemy.scene.yaml
        overrides:
          body:
            alpha: 0.5
      - id: enemy_2
        x: 64
        "y": 40
        scene: scenes/enemy.scene.yaml
        overrides:
          torch:
            x: -6
```

A scene with `inherits` starts as a copy of its base and changes only what it declares:

- `name`, `backgroundColor`, the virtual size and `camera` replace the base's when set, and `tilemaps` entries are added or replaced by name.
- `overrides` changes inherited objects by ID, nested children and instanced objects (`enemy_1/body`) included.
- `layerOverrides` changes inherited layers by name.
- A layer named like one of the base's adds its objects to that layer and may set nothing else; any other layer is added after the base's layers.

An inherited object or layer cannot be removed, and its ID cannot change.

```yaml
# scenes/fast_enemy.scene.yaml: the enemy above, twice as fast
version: 2
inherits: scenes/enemy.scene.yaml
overrides:
  body:
    properties:
      speed: 80
```

An override sets any object field except `id`, `children`, `scene` and `overrides`; a field it leaves out keeps the base's value, and a value of `0` or `false` is still a change. `components` and `properties` are replaced key by key, keeping the object's other keys.

`Resolve(doc, scenes)` loads the referenced files through a `SceneResolver`, such as `FSScenes{FS: os.DirFS(root)}`, and returns a self-contained copy in which no object instances anything and nothing is inherited. It loads each file once per call and never changes `doc` or the documents the resolver returns. It refuses a cycle of scenes that instance or inherit each other, a chain deeper than `MaxSceneNesting` files, a result of more than `MaxResolvedObjects` objects, an override that names no object or layer of the scene it changes, and a result that does not validate, such as one whose prefixed IDs collide with other IDs. `Build` resolves a scene itself when its `AssetResolver` also implements `SceneResolver`, and `BuiltScene.Doc` is then the resolved copy; `SpawnEntities` and `BuildNodes3D` refuse a scene that still refers to others, so resolve it first.

```go
type assets struct {
	sceneformat.AtlasMap
	sceneformat.FSScenes
}

built, err := sceneformat.Build(level, assets{atlases, sceneformat.FSScenes{FS: os.DirFS("project")}})
```

In the editor, `PlaceScene(layer, path, x, y)` places an instance of a scene file as an undoable command, with an ID named after the file (also triggered by dragging a scene file from the File Browser onto the viewport, clicking its `+` button, or pressing **Place** with a scene file selected; registered prefabs in the Asset Browser can likewise be clicked or dragged onto the viewport via `PlacePrefab`). The editor's `Resolver` must implement `SceneResolver`; `PlaceScene`, and `PlaceObject` for any object tree that names a `scene`, resolve the scene as it would be with the instance placed and refuse it when that fails: a missing or invalid file, an override that names no object or holds a bad value, or IDs that collide once instances are expanded. Set the editor's `ScenePath` to the open scene's path from the project root (`OpenYAML` clears it) so that an instance leading back to the open scene, directly or through other files, is refused as a scene cycle before the saved file instances itself. Placing an instance raises a version 1 scene to version 2 (which Undo restores). New IDs are checked against the IDs the scene gets from its base and its instances as well as its own.

Both the 2D and 3D viewports and the scene tree (`SceneTreeView`) let you select any instanced child or inherited object directly (shown muted in the scene tree with an `inst`/`base` tag and an override dot when modified). Editing its transform, sprite, material, particles, 3D light/camera/occluder/mesh/model/GridMap, custom properties, or component fields records an undoable override on the owning instance (`ObjectDoc.Overrides`, keyed by its ID inside the instanced scene) or on the inheriting scene (`SceneDoc.Overrides`, leaving the base file unchanged), and refuses edits whenever `sceneformat.Resolve` fails. Editing an inherited layer's visibility, space, parallax, or repeat records `SceneDoc.LayerOverrides`. The inspector shows an instance/inherited bar with the source scene path, instance count, and override count, marks each overridden field with a warning dot (whose tooltip shows the source value) and a per-field **Revert** button (`RevertObjectOverrideField` / `RevertLayerOverrideField`), and offers **Revert All** (`RevertObjectOverrides` / `RevertLayerOverrides`, pruning empty override maps so the saved YAML stays clean), **Open Scene** / **Open Base Scene** (`OpenSourceScene` / `OpenInstancedScene` / `OpenBaseScene`), **Apply to Source** (`ApplyOverridesToSource`, writing an instance's overrides back to its source scene file and clearing them on the instance in one undo step that restores the source file on Undo), and **Make Local** (`MakeInstanceLocal`, replacing a scene instance with a local copy of its resolved objects and prefixed connections in one undo step). Play runs a resolved copy of the scene, so changes made through `BuiltObject.Doc` show in the editor's document until Stop restores it.

### Example

```yaml
# yaml-language-server: $schema=https://game.nsgo.dev/schemas/scene.v2.json
version: 2
name: Cave Room
virtualWidth: 320
virtualHeight: 180
backgroundColor: "#141824"
camera:
  x: 160
  y: 90
  zoom: 1
  bounds:
    x: 0
    y: 0
    width: 320
    height: 180
tilemaps:
  floor_grid:
    tileWidth: 16
    tileHeight: 16
    width: 4
    height: 2
    tileset: cave_tiles
    data: [1, 2, 2, 1, 1, 0, 0, 1]
layers:
  - name: floor
    space: world
    order: 0
    tilemap:
      ref: floor_grid
  - name: actors
    space: world
    order: 10
    objects:
      - id: hero
        name: Hero
        prefab: prefabs/hero
        x: 48
        y: 40
        originX: 8
        originY: 8
        z: 5
        sprite:
          atlas: actors
          frame: hero
        components:
          collider: { w: 12, h: 14 }
        properties:
          spawn: true
  - name: hud
    space: screen
    order: 100
    objects:
      - id: heart_1
        x: 8
        y: 8
        sprite:
          atlas: ui
          frame: heart
```

### Loading, building and drawing in Go

```go
doc, err := sceneformat.Parse(yamlBytes)
if err != nil {
	return err
}

built, err := sceneformat.Build(doc, sceneformat.FSAssets{
	FS: os.DirFS(projectDir), // YAML tilesets, Tiled and LDtk maps
	Atlases: sceneformat.AtlasMap{
		"cave_tiles": tilesAtlas,
		"actors":     actorsAtlas,
		"ui":         uiAtlas,
	},
})
if err != nil {
	return err
}

// Each update step:
built.Update(dt)
// In a core.Canvas's Draw callback:
built.Draw(d)
```

`Parse` rejects unknown YAML keys, trailing documents, unsupported versions, newer-version fields in older scene versions (version 2 fields in version 1, version 3 parallax/repeat fields in versions 1 and 2, version 4 `environment`/`light`/`camera` fields in versions 1–3), multiple `current: true` 3D cameras, scene references that are not slash-separated paths from the project root, overrides without the `scene` or `inherits` they change, duplicate layer names or object IDs (nested children included), object trees deeper than `MaxObjectDepth`, invalid spaces, malformed colors, and non-finite or out-of-range transforms, light/camera/environment parameters, parallax factors, or repeat periods. `Encode` validates and emits deterministic 2-space YAML that round-trips through `Parse` without loss.

### Schema

The scene format's JSON Schemas are published at `https://game.nsgo.dev/schemas/scene.v1.json` (`sceneformat.SchemaURL`), `https://game.nsgo.dev/schemas/scene.v2.json` (`sceneformat.SchemaURLV2`, for scenes that instance or inherit other scene files, name glTF models, or hold layered grids or LDtk levels), `https://game.nsgo.dev/schemas/scene.v3.json` (`sceneformat.SchemaURLV3`, for version 3 scenes with parallax and repeating layers), and `https://game.nsgo.dev/schemas/scene.v4.json` (`sceneformat.SchemaURLV4`, for version 4 scenes with 3D lights, environment, and 3D cameras). `Encode` starts every file with the `# yaml-language-server: $schema=…` line for its version (`sceneformat.SchemaCommentFor`), so VS Code's YAML extension and other editors with a YAML language server validate and complete a scene as it is written. See [Schemas for YAML files](schemas.md).

### Merging scene files in Git (#1058)

`sceneformat.Merge(base, ours, theirs)` performs an identity-based three-way merge of scene documents: layers are matched by `name`, objects by `id` anywhere in the tree (including across reparenting and layer moves), tilemaps and overrides by key, and connections by `(from, event, to, handler)`. Individual fields—including nested `components`, `properties`, `overrides`, `camera` and `args` maps—merge one by one. The schema `version` merges monotonically to the highest version across `base`, `ours` and `theirs` (never below `base`) so branches that independently raise a scene's version do not conflict, and clean merges are validated with `Validate` and written with `Encode`.

When both sides edit the same field differently, one side deletes what the other modified, both add different items under the same ID, reparenting conflicts or forms a cycle, or the combined scene fails `Validate`, `MergeResult.ConflictDoc(path)` produces a versioned, deterministic YAML `ConflictDoc` (`version: 1`, read and written with `ParseConflict` / `EncodeConflict`) naming every conflict's object, field, and `base`/`ours`/`theirs` values alongside the list of cleanly merged items. Projects wire this into Git through `.gitattributes` (`*.scene.yaml merge=nsgo-scene`) and `go tool game merge -setup` (run once per clone, verified with `go tool game merge -check`; the editor also checks this when opening a project in a Git repository and offers one-click setup from a banner; see [`game merge`](cli.md#merge) and [Scene merge conflict report](formats.md#scene-merge-conflict-report)).

### Component types

A `ComponentRegistry` declares, in Go and without reflection, the component types scene files may use: each type's name and documentation, and each field's name, kind (`FieldBool`, `FieldInt`, `FieldFloat`, `FieldString`, `FieldStringList`, `FieldPoint`, `FieldRect`, `FieldCircle` or `FieldPolygon`), optional allowed `Enum` values (for `FieldString` fields), default, optional inclusive range, optional `Step` for the inspector and documentation. `Register` refuses empty or duplicate names, ranges or steps on non-numeric fields, `Enum` on non-`FieldString` fields, a minimum above the maximum, a fractional range or step on a `FieldInt`, a negative step, and a default of the wrong kind or out of range.

The four 2D shape kinds (`FieldKind.IsShape`) describe world geometry in an object's local coordinate space and are also published as `Point`, `Rect`, `Circle` and `Polygon` definitions in `scene.v1.json`, `scene.v2.json` and `scene.v3.json`:

| Field kind | Go type | YAML form | Validation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `FieldPoint` | `sceneformat.Point` | `{x, y}` | Finite `x`, `y`; unknown keys refused |
| `FieldRect` | `sceneformat.Rect` | `{x, y, width, height}` | Finite numbers, `width >= 0`, `height >= 0`; unknown keys refused |
| `FieldCircle` | `sceneformat.Circle` | `{x, y, radius}` | Finite numbers, `radius >= 0`; unknown keys refused |
| `FieldPolygon` | `[]sceneformat.Point` | `[{x, y}, ...]` | Sequence of valid finite points |

`ParsePoint`, `ParseRect`, `ParseCircle` and `ParsePolygon` normalize decoded YAML values into their Go types, and `MarshalYAML` emits alphabetical keys so saved shape fields round-trip unchanged.

```go
reg := sceneformat.NewComponentRegistry()
reg.MustRegister(sceneformat.ComponentType{
	Name: "body",
	Doc:  "A moving body that collides with the level.",
	Fields: []sceneformat.Field{
		{Name: "solid", Kind: sceneformat.FieldBool, Default: true, Doc: "Blocks other solid bodies."},
		{Name: "speed", Kind: sceneformat.FieldFloat, Default: 32.0, Min: sceneformat.Float64(0), Max: sceneformat.Float64(500), Doc: "Top speed in points per second."},
		{Name: "layer", Kind: sceneformat.FieldInt, Default: 1, Min: sceneformat.Float64(0), Max: sceneformat.Float64(31), Doc: "Collision layer."},
	},
})

doc, err := sceneformat.ParseWith(data, sceneformat.ParseOptions{File: "scenes/room.scene.yaml", Components: reg})
```

With a registry, `ParseWith` checks the components of every object, nested children included, and refuses an unregistered component type, a misspelled or unknown field, a value of the wrong kind (a `FieldInt` refuses `3.0`; a `FieldFloat` accepts an integer), a non-finite number and a value out of range. The error is a `*ComponentError` carrying the file, line and column of the offending key or value, the object ID, component and field, and lists the registered names:

```text
scenes/room.scene.yaml:11:13: sceneformat: object "hero" component "body" field "sped": unknown field (registered: solid, speed, layer)
```

Defaults are not written into the document, so loading and saving a scene changes nothing; `reg.Resolve("body", fields)` returns every field with its default filled in. `ValidateComponents` checks a document built in Go (without positions), and `CheckField` checks one value and normalizes it to `bool`, `int`, `float64` or `string`. `Parse` without a registry keeps `components` free-form.

In the editor, `Editor.SetComponentRegistry(reg)` checks the open scene's components and, as `OpenFile` does, its connections, then makes `OpenFile` report component errors with the file and line, and makes `SetComponentField` and `PlaceObject` refuse fields the registry does not allow. Changing the registry clears undo/redo history, so an undone edit the new registry refuses cannot be redone. The inspector lists every registered field of a component in declaration order, including fields the scene leaves unset, which show their default; the label's tooltip gives the field's documentation, kind, range and default, enum fields show a selector, string-list fields edit comma-separated items, and numeric inputs are bounded by the range and step by the field's `Step` (1 for an int, 0.1 for a float when unset). Floats are shown at full precision, and an edit the registry refuses is reported in the status bar. Components without a registered type, or every component when no registry is set, are shown from the values in the file as before (with the built-in physics component schemas available automatically in the inspector).

### Built-in physics components (#817)

`sceneformat` ships six versioned built-in physics components (`PhysicsComponentVersion = 1`), registered together with `RegisterPhysicsComponents(reg)` or `PhysicsComponentRegistry()`:

| Component | Dimension | Key fields |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `body2d` | 2D | `version` (`1`), `kind` (`static`, `kinematic`, `dynamic`), `mass`, `gravityScale`, `vx`, `vy`, `friction`, `restitution`, `linearDamping`, `angularDamping`, `material` |
| `collider2d` | 2D | `version` (`1`), `shape` (`box`, `circle`), `width`, `height`, `radius`, `offsetX`, `offsetY`, `layer`, `mask` (`[]string`), `isTrigger`; declares events `enter`, `stay`, `exit` |
| `trigger2d` | 2D | `version` (`1`), `shape` (`box`, `circle`), `width`, `height`, `radius`, `offsetX`, `offsetY`, `layer`, `mask` (`[]string`); declares events `enter`, `stay`, `exit` |
| `body3d` | 3D | `version` (`1`), `kind` (`static`, `kinematic`, `dynamic`, `trigger`), `mass`, `gravityScale`, `vx`, `vy`, `vz`, `friction`, `restitution`, `linearDamping`, `angularDamping`, `lockRotation`, `layer`, `mask` (`[]string`), `material`, `physicsMaterial`; declares events `enter`, `stay`, `exit` |
| `collider3d` | 3D | `version` (`1`), `shape` (`box`, `sphere`, `capsule`, `cylinder`, `convex`, `trimesh`, `heightfield`), `width`, `height`, `depth`, `radius`, `offsetX`, `offsetY`, `offsetZ`, `rotX`, `rotY`, `rotZ`, `terrain`, `maxVertices`, `layer`, `mask` (`[]string`), `isTrigger`; declares events `enter`, `stay`, `exit` |
| `trigger3d` | 3D | `version` (`1`), `shape` (`box`, `sphere`, `capsule`, `cylinder`, `convex`), `width`, `height`, `depth`, `radius`, `offsetX`, `offsetY`, `offsetZ`, `rotX`, `rotY`, `rotZ`, `maxVertices`, `layer`, `mask` (`[]string`); declares events `enter`, `stay`, `exit` |

- **Named collision layers**: `layer` and `mask` reference human-readable layer names declared under `physics.layers` in [`game.yaml`](project.md) instead of raw bitmasks (on `collider2d`, `trigger2d`, `body3d`, `collider3d`, and `trigger3d`). Pass `ParseOptions{File: ..., PhysicsLayers: proj.PhysicsLayerTable()}` to `sceneformat.ParseWith` (done automatically by `project.File.LoadAutoloads` and `Editor.OpenProject`): any misspelled or unknown layer name fails to load with a `*loaderr.Error` carrying the exact file path, 1-based line, column, field path, and allowed layer names.
- **Building and stepping (`BuildPhysics`)**: `ps, err := sceneformat.BuildPhysics(built, proj.PhysicsSettings(), lib)` constructs the matching `physics.World`, `physics.RigidWorld`, and `physics3d.World` (applying project gravity, solver iterations, named `Layer`/`Mask` bitmasks, optional `*.physics.yaml` files via `PhysicsMaterialResolver`, glTF/primitive meshes for `convex` and static `trimesh` colliders via `ModelResolver` or `FileResolver`, and `*.terrain.yaml` heightfields for static `heightfield` colliders via `TerrainResolver` or `FileResolver`, recorded in `ps.Bodies3D`, `ps.TriMeshes3D`, and `ps.Heightfields3D`). Calling `ps.Step(dt)` steps 2D kinematic and rigid bodies, 2D triggers, and 3D bodies and triggers, writes updated local poses back onto `BuiltObject.Doc`, and dispatches `PhysicsTriggerEvent` (`TriggerEnter`, `TriggerStay`, `TriggerExit`) to `ps.OnTrigger` and any attached `ps.Signals` (`enter` / `stay` / `exit`).
- **Play-in-editor, viewport debug outlines, 3D collider handles & fit to bounds (#902)**: `Editor.PlayIn` automatically builds and steps `PhysicsScene` (`Editor.PlayPhysics`, `Editor.PlayTriggerEvents`, `Editor.OnPlayTrigger`) so a scene with a player body, wall colliders, and a coin trigger plays in the editor with collision and trigger events without writing game collision code. While `Editor.ShowPhysicsDebug` is enabled (the default; toggle with `SetShowPhysicsDebug`), both the 2D and 3D viewports render wireframe outlines for every `collider2d`, `trigger2d`, `collider3d`, and `trigger3d` (including cylinders, convex hulls, triangle meshes, and terrain heightfields). Switch the 3D gizmo to `Gizmo3DCollider` (`View > 3D Gizmo > Collider`, the Command Palette, or shortcut `shift+c`) to drag collider size handles (`ColliderHandles3D`, `PickGizmo3D`, `BeginGizmoDrag3D`, `UpdateGizmoDrag3D`, `EndGizmoDrag3D`), committing each drag as a single undo step; press **Fit to Model Bounds** in the Inspector (or call `Editor.FitCollider3DToBounds`) to fit a collider's size and local offset to its mesh or glTF model bounds in one undo step.

### Built-in 2D lighting and occluder components (#887)

`sceneformat` ships two versioned built-in 2D lighting components (`Lights2DComponentVersion = 1`), registered together with `RegisterLights2DComponents(reg)` or `Lights2DComponentRegistry()` (and available automatically in the editor's inspector):

| Component | Key fields |
| --- | --- |
| `light2d` | `version` (`1`), `kind` (`point`, `spot`), `color` (CSS color), `radius`, `intensity`, `falloff`, `direction` (radians, added to object rotation), `angle` (spotlight cone spread in radians), `castShadows`, `enabled`, `offsetX`, `offsetY` |
| `occluder2d` | `version` (`1`), `shape` (`rect`, `polygon`, `tilemap`), `width`, `height`, `offsetX`, `offsetY`, `points` (`[][2]float64`, flow YAML `[[x, y], ...]`), `layer`, `tileLayers` (`[]string`), `enabled` |

- **Automatic `lights2d.Scene` build**: `sceneformat.Build` turns `SceneDoc.AmbientColor`, every `light2d` and `occluder2d` on visible world-space layers, and any `shape: "tilemap"` occluders (derived from tilemap collision shapes via [`tileshape.View`](tileshape.md)) into `BuiltScene.Lights2D` (`*lights2d.Scene`). `BuiltScene.Draw` renders `Lights2D` over world-space layers under the scene camera transform (before screen-space HUD layers) unless `SkipLights2D` is set; call `BuiltScene.SyncLights2D()` after moving lights or occluders at runtime.
- **2D lighting preview & interactive handles**: **View > 2D Lighting Preview** or the Command Palette (`Editor.ShowLightingPreview`, `SetShowLightingPreview`, command `CmdToggleLightingPreview`) toggles live `lights2d.Scene` shadows and ambient darkness in edit mode. Selecting a `light2d` object shows interactive handles for its radius (`Handle2DLightRadius`), spotlight cone angle (`Handle2DLightSpotAngle`), and spotlight aim direction (`Handle2DLightSpotDirection`). Selecting an `occluder2d` object shows edge handles for `rect` occluders (`Handle2DOccluderRectN/E/S/W`) and draggable vertex (`Handle2DOccluderVertex`) and edge-midpoint insertion (`Handle2DOccluderEdgeMid`) handles for `polygon` occluders (`SetOccluderPolygonPoints`, `InsertOccluderVertex`, `RemoveOccluderVertex`, `ConvertOccluderToPolygon`). Every drag records one undoable step, Escape cancels it, and `SetAmbientColor` (or the Scene Lighting section in the Inspector) edits `ambientColor` undoably.

### Built-in 2D navigation region components (#888)

`sceneformat` ships a versioned built-in 2D navigation region component (`NavRegion2DComponentVersion = 1`), registered with `RegisterNavRegion2DComponent(reg)` or `NavRegion2DComponentRegistry()` (and available automatically in the editor's inspector and `scene.v1.json` / `scene.v2.json` / `scene.v3.json`):

| Component | Key fields |
| --- | --- |
| `navregion2d` | `version` (`1`), `mode` (`walkable`, `obstacle`), `points` (`[][2]float64`, flow YAML `[[x, y], ...]`), `offsetX`, `offsetY`, `subtractTileCollision` (`bool`), `tileLayer` (`string`), `tileLayers` (`[]string`), `agentRadius` (`float`, `>= 0`), `enabled` (`bool`) |

- **Automatic `pathfind.NavMesh` build (`BuildNavMesh2D`)**: `sceneformat.Build` collects every enabled `navregion2d` on visible world-space layers (normalizing polygon winding so overlapping walkable regions union and obstacle cutouts subtract cleanly), optionally subtracts solid tile collision geometry from scene tilemaps (via [`tileshape.View`](tileshape.md) and non-rectangular solid tile shapes), attaches any authored 2D `nav_link` components, bakes convex polygons with [`game/pathfind/navbake`](navbake.md), and populates `BuiltScene.NavBake2D` (`*navbake.Mesh`) and `BuiltScene.NavMesh2D` (`*pathfind.NavMesh`). Scenes with no `navregion2d` components keep both fields `nil`; call `BuiltScene.SyncNavMesh2D()` after runtime edits.
- **2D NavMesh overlay, polygon handles & Test Path tool**: The 2D viewport renders the built `NavBake2D` convex polygons, shared portals, and authored `navregion2d` outlines whenever `Editor.ShowNavMesh2D` is enabled (default `true`, toggled via **View > 2D NavMesh Overlay**, the 2D viewport's **Overlays** popover, `CmdToggleNavMesh2D`, or `SetShowNavMesh2D`). Selecting a `navregion2d` object exposes draggable vertex handles (`Handle2DNavRegionVertex`) and edge-midpoint insertion handles (`Handle2DNavRegionEdgeMid`) plus `PlaceNavRegion2D`, `InitNavRegionPolygon`, `SetNavRegionPoints`, `InsertNavRegionVertex`, and `RemoveNavRegionVertex`; dragging updates the built mesh overlay live and commits one undoable step on release (Escape cancels). Selecting the **Test Path** tool (`ToolNavTestPath`, **View > Tools > Test Nav Path**, or command `CmdToolNavTestPath`) lets you press start and end points in the viewport to draw the exact path `BuiltScene.NavMesh2D.FindPath` finds (recomputed live as vertices or tiles change, and cleared with Escape or `ClearNavPathPreview2D`).

### Built-in timer component (#1362)

`sceneformat` ships a built-in timer component, the text counterpart of Godot's `Timer` node, registered with `RegisterTimerComponent(reg)` or `TimerComponentRegistry()` (and available automatically in the editor's inspector and the scene JSON schemas):

| Component | Key fields |
| --- | --- |
| `timer` | `seconds` (`1`, `>= 0`), `oneShot` (`false`), `autostart` (`false`); declares the event `timeout` and the handlers `start` and `stop` |

- **Running on the game clock**: `Signals.Update(dt)` advances every timer of the connected scene once per update step — call it in `game.Loop`'s `Update` before `Flush` — and enqueues each `timeout` for that `Flush`, in document order, so a scene wires a timer to any handler with no Go code and timer order stays deterministic. A one-shot timer fires once and stops; a repeating one carries the overshoot into the next period, so it does not drift; `seconds: 0` fires once per update step. The timer of an object whose entity has been destroyed (`SpawnConnected`) stops.
- **`start` and `stop` handlers**: connecting any event to a timer's `start` restarts it from its full `seconds`, and to `stop` halts it without resetting, so a button can arm a door's timer in the file.

### Connections (#490)

A scene file connects an event one object emits to a handler of another object, as Godot's `[connection]` entries in a `.tscn` file do, so a button opens a door with no Go code that names either of them:

```yaml
layers:
  - name: props
    objects:
      - id: button
        components:
          button: {}
      - id: door
        components:
          door: {}
connections:
  - from: button
    event: pressed
    to: door
    handler: open
    args:
      speed: 2.5
```

| Field | Type | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `from` | `string` | Required ID of the object that emits the event. |
| `event` | `string` | Required event name, such as `pressed`, or `button.pressed` to say which component when several components of the object declare it. |
| `to` | `string` | Required ID of the object whose handler runs; it may be `from` itself. |
| `handler` | `string` | Required handler name, such as `open`, or `door.open`. |
| `args` | map | Optional values for the handler's params by name, each a bool, a number or a string; a param left out takes its default. |
| `once` | `bool` | Disconnects after the first time the event fires, as Godot's one-shot connections do. |

The events and handlers come from the component registry: a `ComponentType` lists the `Events` an object with that component emits and the `Handlers` it can run, each handler with typed `Params` (declared and checked like fields) and the `Func` that runs it. The game registers what each kind of component does once; the scene files decide which objects are wired together.

```go
reg.MustRegister(sceneformat.ComponentType{
	Name:   "button",
	Events: []sceneformat.Event{{Name: "pressed", Doc: "The player pressed the button."}},
})
reg.MustRegister(sceneformat.ComponentType{
	Name:   "door",
	Fields: []sceneformat.Field{{Name: "open", Kind: sceneformat.FieldBool}},
	Handlers: []sceneformat.Handler{{
		Name:   "open",
		Params: []sceneformat.Field{{Name: "speed", Kind: sceneformat.FieldFloat, Default: 1.0, Min: sceneformat.Float64(0)}},
		Func: func(c sceneformat.Call) {
			c.Target.Components["door"].(map[string]any)["open"] = true
		},
	}},
})

doc, err := sceneformat.ParseWith(data, sceneformat.ParseOptions{File: "scenes/hall.scene.yaml", Components: reg})
signals, err := sceneformat.Connect(doc, reg, game)
// Where the game handles a press on an object with a button component:
err = signals.Emit(objectID, "pressed", nil)
```

`Connect(doc, reg, ctx)` checks the scene's components and connections and returns a `*Signals`. `Signals.Emit(from, event, payload)` calls the handler of every connection from that event in the order the file lists them, skipping a `once` connection that has run. Each handler gets a `Call`: the `Signals` (so it can emit further events, nested at most `MaxEmitDepth` levels), the `ctx` given to `Connect` (such as the game's state or a `*BuiltScene`), the source and target `*ObjectDoc` in the scene, the components and names of the event and handler, `Args` holding every param (the connection's value or the default) and the `payload`. `Emit` refuses an object the scene does not hold, an event none of its registered components declares, and a bare name two of its components declare (write `component.event`). `Signals` is a snapshot of the scene's objects as `Connect` finds them: after adding or removing objects in `doc`, which may also move the others in memory, call `Connect` again. A scene that instances or inherits other scene files is connected after `Resolve` (or through `BuiltScene.Doc`), and `Signals` is not safe for concurrent use.

`Parse` checks a connection's form: all four names set, `event` and `handler` written as a name or `component.name`, arguments that are bools, finite numbers or strings, no connection repeated, and, in a scene that refers to no other scene file, `from` and `to` naming its objects. With a registry, `ParseWith` also checks the names against it and reports a `*ConnectionError` with the file, line and column: an event or handler no registered component of the object declares, a bare name two of its components declare, an argument the handler has no param for, and an argument of the wrong kind or out of range. The error lists what the object does declare:

```text
scenes/hall.scene.yaml:16:14: sceneformat: connection 0 ("button" pressed -> "door" opne): no registered component of object "door" declares handler "opne" (it has: door.open)
```

`ValidateConnections` makes the same checks on a resolved document without positions. `Register` refuses an event or handler with an empty name, a dot in its name or a name its type already uses, and handler params on the same terms as fields; a registry used only to check files, such as an editor's, may leave `Func` unset, which `Connect` refuses.

`Resolve` carries connections along: an instanced scene's connections come with the instance, the objects they name prefixed like its objects' IDs (`door_1/button`), so every copy of a prefab keeps its own wiring; an inheriting scene keeps its base's connections and adds its own after them. A scene may connect objects of the scenes it instances or inherits, and those names are checked once it is resolved. In the editor, `DeleteObject` also removes the connections from or to the deleted object and its descendants, and undoing the delete puts them back.

#### Queued events

`Emit` runs the handlers before it returns, so a handler that emits runs the next handlers inside itself. To keep a step's order plain, emit with `Signals.Enqueue(from, event, payload)` instead and call `Signals.Flush()` once per step, such as at the end of `game.Loop`'s `Update`:

```go
loop.Update = func(dt time.Duration) {
	updateSystems(dt) // the game's own input and systems, which call signals.Enqueue
	if err := signals.Flush(); err != nil {
		log.Print(err)
	}
}
```

`Enqueue` refuses what `Emit` refuses before it holds anything, so a misspelled event fails where it is raised. `Flush` runs the events enqueued before it began, in the order they were enqueued, each as `Emit` runs it; an event a handler enqueues, its own included, waits for the next `Flush`, so one `Flush` always ends and a handler never re-enters itself. `Flush` from a handler is refused, and `Pending` says how many events wait. A handler's panic passes through `Flush`, and the events after the one that panicked stay queued for the next `Flush`. Once the queue has grown to a step's events, a step allocates nothing more than emitting them would.

#### Listening to events from Go

A scene's connections are declared in the file; the code side of the same events — audio, analytics, achievements, UI — subscribes from Go instead, as `Signal.connect` does in Godot, without a target object or a connection line per scene:

```go
stop, err := signals.Listen("", "opened", func(c sceneformat.Call) {
	playSound("door_open")
}, sceneformat.ListenOptions{})
defer stop()
```

`Signals.Listen(from, event, fn, opts)` checks `from` and `event` as `Emit` does and refuses an unknown object or event at the call, so a misspelt name fails at once; an empty `from` listens to every object of the scene that declares the event. The listener runs after the file's connections for the same event, in the order the listeners were added, and its `Call` carries the source object, the event and the payload (`Target`, `Component`, `Handler` and `Args` stay unset, a listener not being a connection). `ListenOptions.Once` runs it the first time only (`CONNECT_ONE_SHOT`), `ListenOptions.Deferred` queues each run for the next `Flush` (`CONNECT_DEFERRED`), and the returned `stop` ends the subscription — idempotent and safe to call inside a handler. A listener on an object never runs for it once its entity has been destroyed (`SpawnConnected`), as connections do.

#### Spawning connected entities

`SpawnConnected(doc, world, reg, ctx)` checks the scene as `Connect` does and spawns its objects via `SpawnEntitiesWith` with `reg` (so every registered `ComponentType.Spawn` attaches its typed ECS state and rolls back on error), then returns each object ID's entity and the `*Signals` that connects those entities. Every check runs before the first entity is spawned, so a scene whose connection names a missing object, event, handler or `Func`, or whose components break their types, spawns nothing. `EmitEntity(e, event, payload)` and `EnqueueEntity` address an object by its entity, `Entity(id)` returns an object's entity, and each `Call` carries `SourceEntity` and `TargetEntity`, so a handler reaches the target's components with `entities.Get`. A destroyed entity emits nothing: `Emit`, `Enqueue` and their entity forms refuse it, `Flush` drops the events it enqueued, and connections to it are skipped. A scene that instances or inherits others is spawned after `Resolve`, its instances' connections reaching the instances' entities (`west/door`).

#### Checking connections

`go tool game check` resolves each scene that instances or inherits others. It fails on a scene that does not resolve, such as a cycle or an override naming no object, at the instance or `inherits` line, and on a connection whose `from` or `to` names no object of the resolved scene, at the line that names it. When the project has its own editor command (`cmd/editor` importing `pkg/editor/editorcmd`, or `-tags gameeditor` from `game generate editor`), `go tool game check` runs it with `-check` so `assetcheck.CheckWith` also checks components and every connection's event, handler and handler arguments against the project's registry (plus built-in physics and skeletal components). Without an editor command, `game check` checks objects only and prints a note when scenes contain connections explaining how to enable it with `game generate editor`; a game can also run `assetcheck.CheckWith` directly in its own tests with its registry. In both cases it reports, at the file and line, a connection naming an event, handler or argument its objects do not declare and a component that breaks its type; in a scene that instances or inherits others, it also checks the components its overrides write and every connection of the resolved scene, so an override that breaks a connection the instanced or inherited scene brings, such as one adding a second component that declares the same event, fails at the override:

```go
func TestScenesCheck(t *testing.T) {
	report, err := assetcheck.CheckWith(os.DirFS("."), assetcheck.Options{Components: Components})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatal(err)
	}
	for _, p := range report.Problems {
		t.Error(p)
	}
}
```

```text
scenes/level.scene.yaml:12: sceneformat: connection 0 ("lever" held -> "west/door" open): no registered component of object "lever" declares event "held" (it has: button.pressed)
```

A fault that a scene brings unchanged from a scene it instances or inherits is reported on that file, and one that its overrides cause on the override, so each fault is reported once. Problems carry the line, not the column.

### Inspector (#865)

The inspector sets up an object without editing YAML. Every edit it makes is one undoable step, and each has a method on `Editor` that does the same in Go; while Play is on, the methods return `ErrPlaying` and the controls are disabled. An edit the editor refuses is reported in the status bar.

- **Sprite**: the Sprite field picks an atlas frame from those the asset browser lists (`Assets`), grouped by atlas and searchable, and its clear button unbinds it; the button beside it takes the frame selected in the asset browser. `SetObjectSprite(id, ref)` needs both an atlas and a frame, and with a resolver set the atlas must resolve and hold the frame; a nil `ref` unbinds the sprite.
- **Origin and flip**: `originX` and `originY`, the pivot inside the sprite's frame, are set with `SetObjectOrigin(id, x, y)`, which refuses values that are not finite, and `flipX` and `flipY` with `SetObjectFlip(id, flipX, flipY)`.
- **Material**: the Material field picks one of the open project's `*.material.yaml` files and its clear button names none; `SetObjectMaterial(id, path)` takes a slash-separated path from the project root, or an empty one. A material must load through the editor's resolver, which therefore has to implement `sceneformat.MaterialResolver` (as `resource.Library` does), since the scene could not be built otherwise; with any other resolver the field lists no files and stays enabled only to clear a material the object already names.
- **Model (#893)**: in 3D the inspector shows a **MODEL** summary card (`ObjectModelSummary3D`: offscreen thumbnail, project-relative path, triangle count, and distinct material count) above the searchable Model field (`ProjectModels`: the project's `.gltf` and `.glb` files and the outputs of `game.yaml`'s model targets) whose clear button names none, a **MATERIAL** summary (`ObjectMaterialSummary3D`: material file name with roughness and metallic readouts), a **COLLIDER** summary (`ObjectColliderSummary3D`: e.g. `Convex hull · static`), a **CHILDREN** summary card (`ObjectChildrenSummary3D`) when inspecting a group, and **Ask to add a component…** (`OpenComponentAgentPrompt`). `SetObjectModel(id, path)` refuses a model that does not load and a model on an object that draws a built-in primitive, as the Mesh field is disabled on an object that draws a model; changing the model clears `modelNode` and `modelMesh`, which named parts of the old one, and naming one raises a version 1 scene to version 2, the first that has models, and leaves a later one as it is. Undo puts back all three fields and the version.
- **Properties**: Add Property takes a key and a kind (`string`, `int`, `float` or `bool`) and adds the property holding that kind's zero value (`AddProperty(id, key, kind)`); the field trims the key, and an empty key, one with leading or trailing whitespace or one the object has, is refused. A whole-number float property is saved with a decimal point (`2.0`), so it opens again as a float. Each property's delete button removes it (`DeleteProperty`).
- **Components**: the Add Component field filters the registered types the object lacks, and pressing one adds it holding every registered field at its default, so the file shows what the component does (`AddComponent(id, name)`). Enter adds the type the field names, or the one type it matches. A component the object already has, a name the registry does not know, and a name with leading or trailing whitespace or a `.` (which connections use in `component.event`) are refused; without a registry the field adds a component of any name with no fields. Adding a component is refused, too, when it would declare an event or handler that a connection names bare and another of the object's components declares as well, since the connection would no longer say which it means: write it as `component.event` first. In a scene that instances or inherits others, the check runs on the scene as resolved through the editor's resolver, the connections of the scenes it refers to included; it is skipped when the resolver cannot load scene files or the scene does not resolve and bind even without the new component. Each component's remove button removes it (`RemoveComponent(id, name)`) with every connection that needs it, from the object with an event the component declares or to the object with a handler it declares, named bare or as `component.name`; a bare name is attributed through the component's registered type, so without a registry, or for a type the registry does not know, only connections written as `component.name` are removed. `ComponentConnections(id, name)` lists them, and Undo puts the component and its connections back where they were. A component without a registered type keeps its fields editable from the values in the file; having no schema, a whole-number float in one of its fields is saved as `2` and opens again as an int.
- **Connections (#872)**: the Connections section lists the events declared by the selected object's registered components (`ObjectEvents`) and every outgoing and incoming connection touching it (`ObjectConnections`). Pressing **Connect** opens an inline form to pick an event, choose a target object from the searchable object list (`SceneObjectIDs`) or pick it directly from the 2D/3D viewport or scene tree (`BeginConnectionPick` / `CancelConnectionPick` without losing the current selection), pick one of the target's registered handlers (`ObjectHandlers`), fill the handler's params with typed controls from the component registry, and toggle `once`. Each connection row lets you jump to its other end (`JumpToConnectionOtherEnd`), edit it (`UpdateConnection`), or remove it (`RemoveConnection`). Adding, editing and removing connections (`AddConnection`, `UpdateConnection`, `RemoveConnection`) are undoable commands checked with `sceneformat.Validate` and `sceneformat.ValidateConnections` before reaching `History`: an invalid connection (such as naming a handler the target lacks, a duplicate connection, or a wrong param type or out-of-range value) is refused with the reason shown in the status bar, leaving the scene and undo history unchanged. Without a component registry, existing connections remain visible, jumpable, editable and removable.
- **Scene settings (#871, #884)**: when no object, room, layer, or asset file is selected (`ShowingSceneSettings`), the inspector displays the open scene's own settings instead of an empty state: scene `name` (`SetSceneName`), `virtualWidth` and `virtualHeight` (`SetVirtualSize`), `backgroundColor` via NSGo's color picker (`ui.ColorWell`) and CSS hex input with a clear button (`SetBackground`), and the default 2D `camera` (`SetCamera`, `SetCameraPosition`, `SetCameraZoom`, `SetCameraRotation`) with toggleable clamp `bounds` (`SetCameraBounds`, `ClearCameraBounds`). Each change is validated against `sceneformat.Validate` before touching the document or history—refusing non-positive virtual sizes, zoom, or bounds and invalid color strings in the status bar—updates the viewport live, saves and reloads unchanged, and participates in Undo/Redo. While scene settings are shown in 2D edit mode, the viewport highlights the camera's start view frame and dashed bounds, draws a draggable camera position handle (`CameraHandleCanvasPos`, `HitTestCameraHandle`, `BeginCameraDrag`, `UpdateCameraDrag`, `EndCameraDrag`, `CancelCameraDrag`), and draws 8 corner/edge resize handles on `camera.bounds` (`CameraBoundsHandleCanvasPos`, `HitTestCameraBoundsHandle`, `BeginCameraBoundsDrag`, `UpdateCameraBoundsDrag`, `EndCameraBoundsDrag`, `CancelCameraBoundsDrag`) so dragging a handle resizes `camera.bounds` (and dragging the border or empty interior moves it) live, cancels on Escape, and commits as a single undoable step.
- **Room maps (`*.rooms.yaml`, #884)**: open an existing `*.rooms.yaml` file beside the scene from the file browser (`OpenRoomsProjectFile`, `OpenRoomsFile`, `OpenRoomsYAML`) or create one from the Scene Settings inspector (`NewRoomMap`). All rooms draw over the 2D scene viewport with their IDs, display names, transition tags, and 8 resize handles on the selected room. Create rooms by toggling **Draw Room** (`SetRoomCreateMode`) and dragging in the viewport or pressing **Add Room** (`AddRoom`, `AddRoomAtViewCenter`); select, move, and resize rooms with handles (`HitTestRoom`, `HitTestRoomHandle`, `BeginRoomDrag`, `UpdateRoomDrag`, `EndRoomDrag`, `CancelRoomDrag`) and delete them with the Delete button or Delete/Backspace key (`DeleteRoom`). When **Grid Snap** is enabled (default on, `SetRoomSnapToGrid`), room edges snap to the scene's tile grid (`RoomGridSize`, from the scene's inline or external tilemap or an explicit `SetRoomGridSize`). Selecting a room opens the Room Inspector to edit its `id` (`SetRoomID`), `name` (`SetRoomName`), bounds (`SetRoomRect`), and per-room `transition` (`SetRoomTransition`: inherit, `cut`, `slide`, or `fade` with duration, easing, and fade color), as well as the map-level default transition (`SetRoomMapTransition`). All room edits go through `Editor.History`, participate in `HasUnsavedChanges` and `Save`, and save deterministically via `rooms.Encode` (`SaveRooms`, `SaveRoomsFile`, `SaveRoomsYAML`) so files load with `rooms.Parse` and round-trip unchanged.
- **Layer inspector (#885, #877)**: when a layer is selected with no object selected (for example after pressing a layer's row in the scene tree, choosing **Inspect Layer** in the layers panel context menu, pressing **Set Up Tilemap** in an empty tile palette, or calling `SetActiveLayer`), the inspector shows that layer's parallax factors (`Parallax X`, `Parallax Y`), tiling settings (`Repeat` X/Y, `Repeat W`, `Repeat H`), and **Tilemap** setup. In Go, `SetLayerParallax(layer, x, y)` and `SetLayerRepeat(layer, repeatX, repeatY, width, height)` edit parallax and repeat, and `AddLayerTilemap(layer, tileset, tileW, tileH, w, h)`, `SetLayerTilemapTileset(layer, tileset)`, `SetLayerTilemapTileSize(layer, tileW, tileH)`, `ResizeLayerTilemap(layer, left, right, top, bottom)`, `ExtractLayerTilemap(layer, refName)`, `InlineLayerTilemap(layer)`, and `RemoveLayerTilemap(layer)` set up and edit the layer's tilemap—all undoable through `History`. Growing or shrinking from the left or top edge shifts painted cells and updates `offsetX`/`offsetY` so every painted tile keeps its world position (raising version 1 or 2 scenes to version 3 when a non-zero offset is written, restored by Undo). Invalid sizes and tilesets missing frames for painted tile IDs are refused with a status message. Moving a tilemap into top-level `tilemaps` creates a `ref`, and moving it back inline removes the top-level entry once no other layer or `layerOverrides` references it. Parallax and repeat controls are disabled on screen-space layers; inherited base-owned layers disable all layer inspector edits.

### Projects

The editor also opens a game's [project file](project.md): `OpenProject` reads `game.yaml` and opens its main scene, `EditProject` changes it as an undoable step, and `SaveProject` writes it with the `nsgo.yaml` beside it in step. `go tool game editor <dir>` opens a project directory. With `ProjectAssets` set, as the editor commands of package `editorcmd` set it, opening a project makes `Resolver` a `project.Resolver` over the project's directory: the scene draws the project's own sprite atlases, found by name nearest the open scene as `game check` finds them, the asset browser lists them, `PlaceScene` instances the project's scene files, and materials, physics materials, YAML tilesets and Tiled and LDtk maps load from the project, the tilesets and maps through its `ReadFile` as `FSAssets` reads them. An atlas a scene names is found nearest the file that names it, so a scene file instanced or inherited from another directory draws the atlas `game check` finds for it. The editor reads the atlases and material files again whenever it makes a change that may add, remove or rewrite them, such as installing or removing a pack, moving files, `ReloadAssets` or `RefreshSearch`; saving a scene keeps what it has read. `OpenProjectScene(path, scene)` opens the project with the scene file `scene` in place of its main scene, which `go tool game editor` does for a scene file inside a project's directory, so `ScenePath` names it and an instance of it in itself is refused as a cycle. `MoveFile(from, to)` moves a file or directory of the project and rewrites every reference to it, on disk and in the open scene and project, as `go tool game mv` does ([Stable references](project.md#stable-references)), as one step that Undo takes back and Redo makes again. Undo puts each file the move rewrote back byte for byte, unless it changed since, in which case only its references are rewritten back.

While a project is open, the file browser below the asset browser (`FilesView`) lists its files and directories (`ProjectFiles`), leaving out the directories that `game index` and `game mv` skip too: those whose names start with `.` or `_` and those named `testdata`. Pressing one selects it and puts its path in the field below the list; edit the path and press Move to rename or move it through `MoveFile`, press Open Table on a `.yaml`/`.yml` file to open it in the Data Table editor (`OpenDataTableProjectFile`), or press Find Refs to list every reference to it across the project (`ShowReferences`). Refresh lists the files again and rescans the project search and references index (`RefreshSearch`) after something outside the editor changed them.

## Data Table editor (#918)

The Data Table panel (`DataTablePanelView`) opens versioned YAML data tables ([`datatable`](datatable.md)) from the file browser as a spreadsheet-style grid below the viewport:

- **Columns and kinds**: columns are the union of every row's keys in deterministic first-appearance order (plus any fields declared by a registered Go row type that no row currently sets). Each column's value kind (`string`, `number`, `bool`, `duration`, or `list`) is inferred from its cell values or taken from the registered Go row type; boolean cells render as toggle buttons and scalar/list cells commit on Enter or blur.
- **Row operations and view controls**: add (`AddDataTableRow`), delete (`DeleteDataTableRow`), and reorder (`MoveDataTableRow`) rows, sort by clicking any column header (`SetDataTableSort`), and filter visible rows by case-insensitive substring match (`SetDataTableFilter`). Sorting and filtering affect only the view and never reorder or modify the underlying YAML document.
- **Undo and lossless round-trip**: opening, closing, adding, deleting, reordering, and editing cells all go through the editor's shared `History`. Opening and saving a table without edits leaves the file byte-for-byte identical, and editing a single cell changes only that line while preserving comments, formatting, and each row's key order.
- **Go row-type validation and panel states**: a game's own editor command can bind Go struct row types to slash-separated path or filename patterns with `editor.RegisterDataTableType[T](ed, pattern)` (or `Editor.RegisterDataTableRowType`). Matching tables are checked on open and on every cell or row edit with the exact same rules `datatable.LoadYAML` applies, refusing unknown columns, duplicate fields, and invalid cell values in the grid and showing the `*datatable.CellError` in the dismissible panel banner (`DataTableError` / `ClearDataTableError`) and status bar. Asynchronous table loaders can also toggle the loading card with `DataTableLoading` / `SetDataTableLoading`.

```go
if err := editor.RegisterDataTableType[Enemy](ed, "data/enemies/*.yaml"); err != nil {
	log.Fatal(err)
}
```

### Reviewing an agent's data table changes (#1033)

When an agent proposes changes to one or more data table files (through `Editor.OpenDataTableReview` or the `datatable_review_propose` MCP tool), the editor opens a full-window row-by-row Data Table review (`DataTableReviewView`) using exclusively the editor's design spec tokens:

- **Turn files and empty tables**: the left sidebar lists every data table touched in the turn (`27 of 40 changed`, `new · 0 records`); selecting a newly created empty file shows `"No records — ask the agent to add some"`.
- **Filter chips, search, and sorting**: switch between **All**, **Changed**, **Unchanged**, **Reverted**, and **Yours** chips with live counts (`SetDataTableReviewFilter`), search across all fields (`SetDataTableReviewSearch`), and click any column header (`CycleDataTableReviewSort`) to cycle ascending, descending, and back to file order (reporting `aria-sort` via `DataTableReviewAriaSort`). Numeric columns with changes show a `Δ` header and sort by percentage delta without mutating file order.
- **Old → new cells, in-place edits, and drag-to-reorder**: changed cells render in the Spec `changed` dashed pill showing struck-through old value `→` new value and `Δ%`, while numeric zeros render as `—` (`FormatReviewCellValue`). Each changed record has its own **Revert** / **Restore** button (`ToggleDataTableReviewRecord`). Clicking a cell value edits it in place (`StartDataTableReviewCellEdit`, `SetDataTableReviewCellText`, `StepDataTableReviewCellNumber`) and dragging a row's grip handle reorders it (`ReorderDataTableReviewRecord`); both join the review under **Yours** with a solid accent border and a `you` tag in the right-hand YAML diff pane (`DataTableReviewFocusedYAML`).
- **Agent's check figures**: the agent attaches named figures (`DataTableReviewCheck`: label, value, optional value before the request, and basis) via `SetDataTableReviewChecks` or the `datatable_review_checks` MCP tool. Any person revert, restore, in-place edit, or row reorder marks those figures **Out of date** (`DataTableReviewChecksOutOfDate`) until the agent refreshes them.
- **Pause, lossless Keep, and single-step Undo**: pressing `Esc` or **Editor** pauses the review (`PauseDataTableReview`), keeping autosave paused (`HasOpenReview`) and showing a `"Review paused"` toast with **Resume** plus a pending review card in the Agent panel (`ResumeDataTableReview`). **Keep N of M** (`KeepDataTableReview`, `⌘↵`) starts from the original parsed `datatable.Document` bytes and patches only the kept and edited lines so untouched and reverted lines stay byte-for-byte identical, committing every file in **one atomic Undo step** (`Undo` restores all files byte for byte and removes newly created files). **Revert all** (`RevertAllDataTableReview`, `⌘⌫`) closes the review cleanly without modifying disk and stays undoable with `⌘Z`.

### 2D viewport navigation (#864)

The 2D viewport's canvas fills its panel and shows the scene through the editor's own view (`View2D`: the world point at the canvas's center, a zoom and a rotation), separate from the scene's `camera`, so a level larger than one screen can be seen and edited anywhere. The view is never saved to the scene nor recorded in the undo history.

- A mouse wheel, a pinch, or a touchpad scroll with Control or Command held zooms about the pointer, keeping the world point under it fixed (`ZoomView2DAt`), between `MinView2DZoom` and `MaxView2DZoom`.
- A touchpad's two-finger scroll, a mouse wheel's sideways scroll (Shift+wheel or a tilting wheel), a middle-button drag, a drag with Space held or a two-finger drag on a touch screen pans (`PanView2D`).
- F, or the Frame Selection button, frames the selected object (`FrameSelection2D`, which frames everything when nothing is selected); Home, or Frame All, frames every object of the visible layers, their inline tilemaps and the camera's frame (`FrameAll2D`); Mod+0, or Scene Camera View, returns to the scene camera's view (`ResetView2D`).

Until the author pans or zooms, and again after `ResetView2D` or opening another scene, the view is the scene camera's view: the virtual screen centered in the canvas at the camera's zoom and rotation, following the camera as the scene changes (`View2DFollowsCamera`). In edit mode the whole canvas takes the scene's `backgroundColor`, so objects far from the camera are seen against the game's clear color; it outlines the camera's frame, the virtual screen as the player sees it, and draws the camera's `bounds` dashed. Screen-space layers draw inside that frame. In Play mode only the frame shows, centered at the camera's view whatever view the author left, as in the game; the view does not pan or zoom until Stop brings the author's view back.

Hit testing, placing, moving, tile painting, the selection outline and plugin tools all go through the view, applying each world-space layer's parallax offset (and previewing its repeat tiling) smoothly as the 2D view pans. `CanvasToWorld` and `WorldToCanvas` convert between canvas points and the world; `CanvasToScreen` and `ScreenToCanvas` between canvas points and the scene's virtual screen, where screen-space layers live; `ScreenToWorld` and `WorldToScreen` apply the scene's camera alone, as the game does. `CanvasSize` is the canvas's laid-out size, the scene's virtual size until the viewport lays out (`SetCanvasSize`); the 3D viewport fills the same canvas.

### 2D transform gizmo (#863)

With the Select or Move tool, the 2D viewport edits an object's transform by dragging, as Godot's and Unity's 2D transform tools do:

- A drag on any object selects it and moves it, keeping the offset from where it was grabbed, so the object does not jump to the pointer.
- The selected object carries a gizmo drawn at a fixed size on the canvas: a square at its origin moves it freely; a red and a green arrow move it along its own X and Y axes, which turn with its rotation; a ring around it turns it about its origin; and, once its outline is at least 24 points on a side on the canvas (zoom in on a small object), handles on its corners and edges scale it about its origin, the edges one axis and the corners both.
